   136	
   137	## 6. Build sequence
   138	
   139	Ordered by dependency, not by appetite. Each step names what would make it wrong.
   140	
   141	1. ~~**Signer skeleton — no keys.**~~ **BUILT 2026-08-18 — `signer/`, and it runs.** IPC transport per §3, per-transaction confirmation UI, scoped short-lived authority, refusal of caller-supplied randomness. *Falsified if:* the confirmation renders client-supplied strings rather than parsed transaction fields. **The falsifier is written and passing** — `signer/tests/falsifier.rs::confirmation_never_renders_what_the_client_claimed` sends REVIEW-003b's own attack (an intent claiming *"Send 1 unit to Alice"* while its real fields say 9999 elsewhere) and asserts no client string reaches the screen. 25 tests, all passing (E-73).
   142	
   143	   **The property is enforced by types, not by discipline:** `ParsedTransaction` has one constructor, `parse(&[u8])`, and `confirm::render` takes `&ParsedTransaction` and nothing else, so a client string cannot reach the holder without someone first widening a signature.
   144	
   145	   **Transport is a Unix domain socket, not localhost HTTP** — §3 item 4's preferred option, taken. A browser page cannot open one by any web API, so the XSS-to-spend-oracle path is closed by construction rather than by an `Origin` check. **It does not stop a hostile process running as the same user**, which is in scope and was never going to be stopped there; the confirmation is the boundary.
   146	
   147	   ~~**There is no randomness ledger in this skeleton, deliberately.** Step 4 is not built, and a REVIEW-013 leg warned that scaffolding must not bake in the A1–A3 store semantics.~~ **Both halves are now spent**: step 4 was built later the same day against **A4–A9**, and the warning was heeded — no A1–A3 semantics were ever written. **`txn::serialize`'s length-framed placeholder is also gone**, replaced by canonical XRPL bytes at step 3.
   148	2. ~~**Ledger read path.** Public state, the observer view, issuance and holder objects. Already largely exists in `tools/`.~~ **BUILT AND EXECUTED 2026-08-19 — `signer/src/ledger.rs`.** *Falsified if:* the signer builds a transaction against ledger state it did not read, or a stale read is indistinguishable from a fresh one. *Also falsified if:* it implies amounts are visible or that identities are hidden.
   149	
   150	   **The one line above understated it in the same way step 3's did.** *"Already largely exists in `tools/`"* was true of the reading and false of everything that matters: `tools/` reads the ledger to *print* it, and a signer reads it to *prove against it*. Three things followed that the line did not contain.
   151	
   152	   - **A wrong read is a confidentiality break, not a rejection.** A Send encrypts the amount to the destination's and the issuer's keys, **both read from the ledger**, and rippled verifies against the keys it holds. A substituted key fails the proof with `tecBAD_PROOF` — and a `tec` is **applied to the ledger**, so the ciphertext under the adversary's key is published permanently. The signer does not submit, so it cannot set the `fail_hard` that would prevent it. `CLAIMS.md` **F-23**, read at rippled `3.3.0`/`00a178f`, **not executed**. Consequence: TLS is required for any non-loopback endpoint, and B-15 — whether **one** endpoint is acceptable at all — is open.
   153	   - **Two `"validated"` reads seconds apart land in different ledgers.** Observed live: `account_objects` at 4596692 and `account_info` at 4596693, back to back, because the public endpoint is a cluster (E-103). A snapshot assembled from both is a state that never existed, so the index is pinned once and every later call carries that number.
   154	   - **Staleness had to be *detected*, not bounded.** The window is the confirmation, which a human holds open for minutes. The signer **re-reads after the human confirms** and refuses if any binding field moved (`REFUSED_LEDGER_MOVED`), with a control test proving an ordinary ledger close does not fire it (E-102).
   155	
   156	   **Nine fields left the holder profile**, which now carries secrets and policy only and **refuses any of the nine by name** rather than ignoring it. 168 tests; `tests/ledger_read.rs` is 43 of them and `tests/ledger_devnet.rs` runs the same code against a live node. **On Devnet:** a `ConfidentialMPTSend` built, proved and signed from state the signer read for itself — `tesSUCCESS`, validated, `C63CDCB41183DF55A1CBE9B955AD5EB5C7FE037037AC87EDE31DC4799C5C0EDE` (E-101), then a Convert and **two** Sends on a fresh holder where the second Send's sequence and balance version came from the signer's own read with no help from the driver.
   157	
   158	   **REVIEWED 2026-08-19 (`REVIEW-016`), two legs, and the review found real defects.** ⚠ **Both legs independently found the same one**, which is the strongest signal available: `LastLedgerSequence` was read-index + 20, borrowed from `xrpl.js`'s `autofill` — which runs at *submit* time — while this signer puts a **120-second human confirmation** between building and signing. 20 ledgers is ~60–80s on Devnet, so a holder who took ninety seconds got a transaction that had already expired and the signer signed it. Four more, one leg each: the plaintext rule was bypassable by naming a host `127.attacker.example`; `network_id` was cast `as u32`, so `4294967298` truncated to `2` and passed the Devnet guard; *"one pinned ledger index"* was asked for and **never checked**; and `signer_lists` was read from one of the **two** places rippled puts it, so issue #11's third condition would read as zero against an `apiVersion` 2 endpoint. All fixed with tests, all seven spot-checked by reverting them. **Two leg claims were wrong and are pinned by tests rather than by disposition notes** (E-106).
   159	
   160	   **F-23 is EXECUTED as of 2026-08-19**, not merely read at source: `tecBAD_PROOF` recorded on validated ledger 4603815, and the amount recovered from public ledger data by the party who supplied the wrong key while the intended destination could not decrypt it.
   161	
   162	    **`CLAIMS.md` B-15 was ruled 2026-08-20** (`REVIEW-019`): one endpoint stands for this research artifact **as a named risk acceptance**, with a mandatory disclosure printed whenever it is not loopback. ⚠ **It is a GATE on step 7** — the facility requires loopback, corroboration across independently operated nodes, or a submitter that sets `fail_hard`, and which of the three is a step 7 decision.
   163	
   164	   ⚠ **This is what unblocks step 6.** Issue #11 requires detecting `lsfDisableMaster`, a regular key or a signer list, and that **is** a ledger read. `AccountSecurity` reads all three today. ~~**Step 6 is not built and nothing branches on them yet**~~ — **that became false within the same session and round two of `REVIEW-016` caught it.** `lsfDisableMaster` **is** branched on, in two places: `preflight` refuses outright (`REFUSED_MASTER_KEY_DISABLED`, because a master-key signature on such an account is answered `tefMASTER_DISABLED`), and `binding_differs` refuses when it is set mid-confirmation. **A regular key and a signer list are read and branched on nowhere** — they belong to step 6's keygen question, not to signing, and folding them in would refuse transactions rippled would apply.
   165	3. ~~**Wire the vendored prover into the signer**~~ **BUILT AND EXECUTED 2026-08-18.** Throwaway Devnet keys from `mpt_generate_keypair`. Sends and Converts end-to-end. *Falsified if:* `vendor-verify.sh` fails or the pristine upstream verifier rejects a proof. **Neither happened.**
   166	
   167	   **The falsifier fired and passed.** `signer/tests/prover_pristine.rs` hands proofs this signer generated to `/tmp/wconf/mptool-pristine` — a **separate binary linked against pristine upstream `85dbac7`** — at n=3, n=4 and a zero-amount Send, and all are accepted (E-80). **It can fail:** corrupting one nibble makes the same verifier answer `FAIL`, which is asserted, because a verifier that answers `OK` to everything makes every other assertion vacuous. `vendor-verify.sh` passes, including its `0.4.0-rc4` vs `1.0.4` source-identity check — which is what makes a `1.0.4` verifier the ledger's verifier rather than a local curiosity.
   168	
   169	   **On Devnet:** a `ConfidentialMPTSend` built, proved and signed entirely by `signer/` was accepted — `tesSUCCESS`, validated, `9170912934A842CE90494E66485ADC82739B2276ECD48E2900A1F62A164F9DB0` (E-84). The hash the signer computed matched the node's exactly.
   170	
   171	   **Three things this step turned out to contain that its one line did not say:**
   172	
   173	   - **The FFI could not use the published crate.** `mpt-crypto-sys@0.1.0-beta.1` pins `0.3.0-rc2` and declares seven functions that no longer exist at `1.0.4` (F-18), so the signer declares its own `extern "C"` surface and `build.rs` pins the three header digests. Two guards, both spot-checked in **both** directions: a pristine library fails to **link**, and a one-line header change stops the **build** (E-81).
   174	   - **XRPL serialisation had to be written.** There is no Rust `ripple-binary-codec` that knows Confidential MPT fields, so `signer/src/xrpl.rs` is a new encoder, decoder, address codec and signer — pinned byte-for-byte against `ripple-binary-codec@2.9.0`, `ripple-keypairs@2.0.0` and `ripple-address-codec@5.0.1` (E-83). **ed25519 was not optional**: every account this project holds on Devnet has an `sEd…` seed.
   175	   - **The confirmation had to change, because the amount is not on the wire.** A Send carries no plaintext amount. The confirmation now **decrypts `SenderEncryptedAmount` out of the transaction it is about to sign**, with the holder's own key, and says `*** COULD NOT BE READ ***` rather than guessing. That is strictly stronger than what step 1 achieved, and it is the same property.
   176	
   177	   **REVIEWED 2026-08-18 (`REVIEW-015`), two legs, and the review found real defects.** **Both legs independently found the same one**, which is the strongest signal available: the signer built a **four-participant** proof when an auditor key was configured and serialised only **three** ciphertexts, so every Send on an auditor-enabled issuance would have been unverifiable (F-22). It hid because the Devnet issuance has no auditor and no test fixture had one — **a whole configuration branch with zero coverage, looking fully exercised**. Both legs also found that `Convert` skipped the issuance check `Send` performs. Upheld and fixed: validation ran *after* the randomness reservation, and a test had codified that as intent (E-90); `parse` ignored unrecognised fields while claiming to refuse them, and `MPTAmount` on a Send would have put a **plaintext amount on the confirmation screen** (E-91); and **the step's own falsifier could be aimed at the patched binary and pass** (E-92). **Two findings were wrong and are pinned by tests rather than by a disposition note** (E-93). **A third round** (2026-08-19) was run against this document's own two-round rule, because round two's fixes changed `Convert`'s wire format on the adjudicator's reading of the spec with no second reader. It found `parse` giving `ConvertBack` *Convert's* field set — XLS-0096 §10.3 contradicts that in three places, and a valid ConvertBack would have been **rejected twice over** (E-96). ⚠ **The rule is not overturned; it counts rounds of review, and a fix that introduces new protocol-facing behaviour is a new thing to review.** Adjudicated in `REVIEW-015c`, §8.
   178	4. **Implement the `r`/`ρ` construction** from REVIEW-010c, with its test. ~~**The three amendments A1–A3 are now written into the ruling** (2026-08-18) and the signer must satisfy them: keyed storage so the ledger is not a linkable index into on-chain activity, promotion of expired `pending` entries rather than deletion, and loud degradation rather than bricking on store loss. **They are state-machine rules guarding a cryptographic invariant, so they need adversarial review before the signer is built**, not after.~~ **REVIEWED 2026-08-18 (`REVIEW-013`), and both legs ruled the signer may NOT be built against A1–A3 as they stood.** Six defects, four found independently by both: the store's *set* was unauthenticated so truncation and rollback were undetectable; A1's "pruning bounds the window" contradicted A2's "promote, never delete"; A3's reset was acknowledgeable over IPC and its gap record lived inside the store being reset; A1's protection was overclaimed against the same-host adversary it cited. **Build against A4–A9**, which correct A1–A3 in the same document. The gate this step named is discharged.
   179	
   180	   **BUILT 2026-08-18 — `signer/src/randomness.rs` and `signer/src/store.rs`.** The KDF reproduces all nine of REVIEW-010 Deliverable B's vectors from a **second implementation written from the ruling's prose rather than from the TypeScript** (E-75). The ledger implements A4–A9, with a test per amendment: truncation, rollback, reordering and alteration all detected; identifiers append-only; the reset acknowledgeable only in the signer's own window with the gap record in a **separate** log that survives deleting the store; a cancelled confirmation burns rather than clears; a live reservation is not swept out from under a waiting human. **57 tests pass.**
   181	
   182	   ~~⚠ **The named falsifier for this step — `observer-harness.ts --assert-no-r-reuse` — has NOT been run against a transaction built by this code**~~ **IT HAS NOW, AND IT PASSED (E-85).** It could not be run against the 2026-08-17 holder: that account's history contains **E-63's deliberately broken Sends**, and the assertion fires on them correctly whatever this signer does. So `tools/spike-step3-2026-08-18/devnet-clean-account.ts` built a clean holder — `rnU7af8QRAgE2Qp7qeAYJLgeMmmTcbvX81` — whose every confidential transaction came from `signer/`: one Convert and **two** Sends, because "no reuse" across a single transaction is vacuous. **ASSERTION PASSED: no ElGamal randomness reuse across 7 transactions**, exit 0.
   183	
   184	   **And a second, sharper check the step never named.** `r_entry` commits to `compressed(r·G)`, and `C₁ = r·G` is on the wire — so `signer/src/bin/store-check.rs` takes `C₁` values off the public ledger and confirms the randomness they published is the randomness the ledger recorded. All three **RECORDED, committed**; E-63's reused `C₁` from the other account reports **NOT IN THE LEDGER** (E-86).
   185	
   186	   ~~⚠ **And the signing path does not call either of them yet.**~~ **WIRED 2026-08-18**, as this line said it would be. `session::prepare` now runs the eight steps in the order the rulings require, and `signer/tests/signing_path.rs` is what fails if it does not: a signing appends four records; the recorded identifier is the one for the published `C₁`; **a declined confirmation burns rather than clears**, and so does a refusal after the reservation; five signings never share a `C₁`; two attempts in a byte-identical context still produce different randomness (E-88). ⚠ **One real defect surfaced here and no review found it** — `SigningPubKey` is a signing field and was being added *after* the digest, so the signature covered a serialisation the blob did not contain. Every validator would have rejected it (E-87).
   187	
   188	   **REVIEWED 2026-08-18 (`REVIEW-014`), and the review found real defects.** Two independent legs against the committed diff. One CRITICAL and four HIGH, the two most serious found by one leg each: a full-directory snapshot rollback is undetectable by any local check (now **stated** rather than claimed away); `Store::create` could silently destroy a live ledger; the generation moved *backwards* when the header was destroyed; the audit log was not append-only against truncation — **A4's own defect, reproduced one file over in the same commit**; and A6 item 5's restore-before-reset was missing. All fixed, each with a falsifying test, and two of the fixes were spot-checked by reverting them to confirm the tests fail. Adjudicated in `REVIEW-014c`; the disposition disagrees with one leg on the recovery *direction* and says why. *Falsified if:* `observer-harness.ts --assert-no-r-reuse` ever fires on our own transactions — **a partial falsifier, and deliberately named as one**: it catches `r` reuse after the fact and says nothing about `ρ` lifecycle, entropy-failure handling, or refusal of caller-supplied fields.
   189	5. ~~**— gate — ADR-0001 `Accepted`.** Requires the keygen ruling.~~ **✅ PASSED 2026-08-18.** The gate was never the keygen ruling by the end — that landed on 2026-08-18 and the ADR still did not move. It was the Option 2 re-weigh, discharged in `REVIEW-013c`. **Steps 6 and 7 are no longer blocked by the ADR; they are blocked by their own items in §5.**
   190	6. ~~**Real key generation and onboarding**, per the keygen ruling — which now exists.~~ **BUILT AND EXECUTED 2026-08-20 — `signer/src/keygen.rs`, `signer/src/onboarding.rs`.** ⚠ **The step was under-scoped by the line it used to be, and issue #11 carried the correction**, and that correction was right: the *cryptography* is about fifty lines and its test already passed, while the *onboarding* is a second key-source path, a second backup story and a second flow. *Falsified if:* `tools/spike-keygen-2026-08-18/t-keygen-construction.ts` fails, or onboarding does not warn when the account carries `lsfDisableMaster`, a regular key or a signer list. **Both halves of that falsifier are written and firing** — `tests/keygen_kat.rs` reproduces the spike's vectors from a Rust implementation written from the ruling's prose, and `tests/onboarding.rs` drives all three conditions **individually**.
   191	
   192	   **Four things this step turned out to contain that its line did not say.**
   193	
   194	   - **The interop tests `CLAUDE.md` requires cannot be run as they stand, and the sentence next to them is the load-bearing one.** `test_pok_sk`, `test_elgamal` and `test_elgamal_verify` are C programs over *random* keys, so `tests/keygen_interop.rs` drives their **properties** with a derived key instead. And it drives the caveat: *"a constant `sk_A = 1` passes all three"* is a claim, so `a_constant_sk_of_one_passes_every_upstream_check` runs it and asserts they **pass**. That is what makes the known-answer vectors load-bearing rather than decorative.
   195	   - **`ElGamalKeypair::from_parts` never checked that `pk` was `sk·G`**, and the failure it allowed is the worst one this protocol offers: register a key nobody holds the secret for, encrypt every balance to it, and have every step succeed. `CLAIMS.md` **E-120**. Two existing test fixtures were using a pair that had never corresponded.
   196	   - **A profile can now hold no secret at all.** With `"account_seed": "prompt"` and the derived key source, the seed is typed into the daemon's own window and nothing is on disk — which is the only arrangement here where the ruling's §4 holds (E-123). ⚠ It is **opt-in**, because the Devnet drivers run the daemon without a terminal.
   197	   - **§(e)'s benefit is executable, and it was executed.** `cargo run --bin onboard` in recovery mode takes a seed and the account address and reproduces the key on the ledger (E-119). ⚠ **That is not issue #10.** It shows the seed is sufficient *given the derivation*; #10 is about whether the derivation is reachable at all, and no code discharges it.
   198	
   199	   **On Devnet:** a `ConfidentialMPTConvert` registering a **derived** `HolderEncryptionKey` — `tesSUCCESS`, validated, `39C7834DD71742B4FEE27D521B4953799B70866443478DF6B7C98901EC8A31D9`, holder `rDYMkRMh7o7QfkNaoGvtd7TgFzmt12FEM8` — then a MergeInbox and two Sends, and the registered key **compared against a second, independent TypeScript derivation** through a validated ledger entry (E-118). `observer-harness.ts --assert-no-r-reuse` passed, 7 transactions, exit 0.
   200	
   201	   **REVIEWED 2026-08-20 (`REVIEW-020`), two rounds, four legs, and NOT ONE finding touched the derivation.** Both round-one legs independently walked the ruling's §2 against `signer/src/keygen.rs` and both reported it byte-exact; neither recommended putting an abort path back. **Every finding landed in the onboarding half**, which is what issue #11 predicted. Round one upheld three: the confirmation told a holder at the approval step that the registered key *"can never be recovered"* — **false for a derived key, and false about the only thing deriving buys** (E-125); *"the only time the secret is shown"* printed it twice, with the guarding test asserting a count of two; and the ordering test drove only a clean account. ⚠ **Two round-one claims were wrong and are pinned by tests rather than by notes** — the fifth time.
   202	
   203	   **Round two found no defect in any fix, and two false sentences written about them** — which is the category a second round exists for. ⚠ **One of them had survived a full round of review because it lived in the one disclosure surface that was not a constant with an asserted wording** (E-126): the daemon banner said *"Neither key can be ROTATED: XLS-0096 has no rotation and no recovery"*, and XLS-0096 does not govern XRPL account keys — an account's spending authority **can** be moved, which is the case this signer warns about a few lines below, in the same window, on the same run.
   204	
   205	   ⚠ **What condition 2 buys and where it stops.** The signer warns, flips the recommended source and demands a second typed sentence. It does **not refuse**, because the ruling says *warn and offer*; and none of it defeats an adversary with code execution, who reads `sk_A` from process memory (ruling §8 item 2). **`mlock` is not implemented and is a named gap, not a claim.**
   206	7. **The facility as application logic** — Dedicated Account, single conversion, balance tracking (ADR-0002, TASK ZERO §9). **Say "Dedicated Account", never "vault"**: an XLS-65 `Vault` cannot convert, and the word has already nearly derailed one reading.
   207	
   208	   **OPENED 2026-08-24, and what was built is NOT the facility.** Three things stood in front of it and two were code:
   209	
   210	   - **`ConfidentialMPTConvertBack` — BUILT AND EXECUTED.** The walkthrough's step 9, and a facility whose exit does not exist is not the facility. Its field set is XLS-0096 **§10.3's**, with the text **re-fetched at source** rather than reconstructed from this project's review notes; the three differences from Convert — a **required** 816-byte `ZKProof`, **no `HolderEncryptionKey` field at all**, and a **required** `BalanceCommitment` — each have a negative assertion. Two on Devnet under a **derived** key, both validated, with all four of §10.5's state changes checked against ledger state rather than inferred from `tesSUCCESS` (E-131).
   211	   - **E-76's other half — CLOSED** (E-132). The ruled `r`/`ρ` derivation is no longer *"in the signer, not everywhere"*.
   212	   - **B-15's step-7 gate — ANSWERED 2026-08-25.** Loopback for the facility's reads, `fail_hard` set by whatever submits. ⚠ **Neither is enforced by the signer**, so both are obligations on the build: a non-loopback endpoint anywhere in the facility, or a submitter that omits the flag, silently reopens F-23. ~~**HANDED OVER, NOT DECIDED.**~~ `docs/decisions/DECISION-REQUEST-b15-step7-ledger-source-2026-08-24.md` costs the three options. ⚠ **One of them adds a submitter, and *"the signer reads the ledger and still does not write to it"* is a claim §1 says is load-bearing in both halves.**
   213	
   214	   ⚠ **Building the exit transaction is not passing the gate.** ConvertBack is a transaction type, built under exactly the risk acceptance `REVIEW-019` allows for a research artifact. ~~The Dedicated Account, the drawdowns, the repayments and the balance tracking are what the gate covers and none of them exists.~~ **BUILT 2026-08-25 — `facility/`.** The Dedicated Account, the single conversion, supplier enrolment, drawdowns, repayments, the balance book and retirement in tranches. **81 tests, a real type-check, and four gates** (2026-08-26; the count read *"63 tests, three gates"* until `REVIEW-023` round two, and 63 was stale even at round one's tree, which runs 77). ~~⚠ **NOT EXECUTED — see below.**~~ **EXECUTED 2026-08-25 — 28 validated facility transactions, NOT under B-15's step-7 conditions.** ⚠ **That strike is dated 2026-08-26, because `REVIEW-023` C-6 named this line by file and line and the fix did not reach it** — so this document said *NOT EXECUTED* here and *BUILT AND EXECUTED* ten lines below, for a day, in the document `CLAUDE.md` calls the authority on what is open.
   215	
   216	   **What the build actually consists of**, because "application logic" understates it in the way step 2's one line did:
   217	
   218	   - **`facility/src/loopback.ts` is the only ledger read path**, and it refuses a non-loopback literal **before it connects** — so a misconfigured facility cannot tell a remote host the shape of its reads on the way to failing. ⚠ **Unless the risk has been accepted by name** (`WCONF_ACCEPT_REMOTE_LEDGER_RISK`, E-151), in which case it prints the disclosure and stamps the run `underB15Conditions: false`. It is **stricter than the signer**: only a numeric literal in `127.0.0.0/8` or `::1`, because a name is resolved by something the process does not control. `REVIEW-016`'s two bypasses and round two's unterminated `[::1` are negative test inputs.
   219	   - **`facility/src/submit.ts` is the submission path for everything the facility does**, and it has **no method that can omit `fail_hard`**. The flag's effect is read at source — rippled 3.3.0 `Transactor.cpp:1645-1651`, *"If the TapFailHard flag is set, a tec result must not do anything"*, plus `NetworkOPs.cpp:1833`, which also stops it being kept locally **or relayed**. ⚠ **One deliberate exception exists and is not in that file**: the fail-hard control's second arm submits once *without* the flag, because demonstrating that the flag makes a difference requires an arm that does not set it. ⚠ **`CLAIMS.md` F-25 is why the flag cannot be bound into the transaction at all** — it is an `ApplyFlags` value chosen by the submitter, not a field of the signed blob, which is the structural reason B-15's Part C is a convention.
   220	   - ⚠ **The driver is NOT the web application of §1, and it holds every party's seed.** §1's box says the web tier *"never sees a secret"*; that remains the design and it is not what `facility/run-facility.ts` is. One process plays the bank, the Dedicated Account and every supplier, writes each family seed into a signer profile on disk, and derives every ElGamal secret to reconcile the book. **Every property that depends on three parties on three machines is not demonstrated by this milestone** (`REVIEW-022g` §2).
   221	   - **`tools/check-loopback.ts` is the static half** of the loopback obligation, and `LoopbackLedger.open` is the dynamic half. Neither alone is the property; both together are close, and *"close"* is the honest word.
   222	   - **`facility/src/book.ts` is TASK ZERO §9's balance-tracking requirement** (R-5, F-8), and the run prints what a lost book costs in **search width** rather than in seconds, because P-8 forbids a benchmark number without hardware, parameter set and methodology.
   223	
   224	   ⚠ **BUILT AND EXECUTED 2026-08-25 — 28 validated facility transactions — but NOT under B-15's step-7 conditions.** Every party's decrypted `ConfidentialBalanceSpending` equals the book's prediction, all four registered `HolderEncryptionKey`s are the ruled derivation, the issuance's plaintext `ConfidentialOutstandingAmount` of 2030 is exactly the sum of the three hidden positions, `fail_hard` executed in **both** arms, and `observer-harness --assert-no-r-reuse` passed over 14 transactions. ⚠ **The ledger source was remote**, under the owner's amendment (`CLAIMS.md` **E-151**), so the run is stamped `underB15Conditions: false` and is **not evidence about the loopback obligation**. ⚠ **The first RECONCILIATION refused and that is how F-27 was found**: `ConfidentialMPTConvert` deposits into the **inbox**, not the spendable balance, and the **then-existing** tests could not have caught the mistake because the suite asserts this project's model of the protocol against itself. ⚠ **Both narrowings are round two's.** This sentence said *"the first Devnet transaction"* — the authorize, the public `Payment` and the issuance setup all came first — and *"no unit test could have caught"*, which is false: `facility/tests/book.test.ts` encodes the rule now and fails if `convert` credits `spending`. `REVIEW-023` C-7 ruled both fabricated absolutes **and cited this line**; the fix reached `book.ts` and not this document.
   225	
   226	⚠ **What Option A cost, which is why the amendment exists.** B-15's answer requires loopback, so the facility needs a rippled node syncing Devnet. `tools/loopback-node/` is that node — rippled 3.3.0 at git `00a178f`, **the same commit `CLAIMS.md` F-23 was read at** — and it did **not** finish its initial sync inside the session: a fresh node must acquire a whole account state tree while Devnet keeps closing ledgers, and each attempt aborted before completing (`InboundLedger: … abort good:29130 dupe:822`) with 3 GB fetched and no validated ledger. ~~**No facility transaction has been submitted.**~~ **Struck 2026-08-26 — 28 were, on 2026-08-25**, and `REVIEW-023` C-6 cited this line too. Falling back to the public endpoint was available and was **refused by the coding session** — and then **ruled, taken and disclosed**, because the owner amended B-15's answer the same day (**E-151**). ⚠ **The sentence stated the project's final position and had it backwards.** What survives is the narrow rule it was reaching for: *a coding session may not substitute a ledger source on its own authority.* The whole content of the ruling is that the public endpoint is not sufficient here.
   227	
   228	   ⚠ **A standalone rippled is NOT a fallback, and the reason is a property of this project rather than an accident.** `signer/src/ledger.rs` hardcodes `DEVNET_NETWORK_ID = 2` and *"refuses every network but Devnet, at any phase, for any reason"*. A local network would have to claim `network_id` 2 to drive the signer — a local ledger presenting itself as Devnet, which is exactly the substitution this project forbids. **So Option A costs a full Devnet sync and there is no rehearsal network.** That was not in the decision request's cost table.
   229	
   230	   ⚠ **One thing step 7 turned up that ADR-0002 does not contain, and it is a disclosure error in our own document**: `CLAIMS.md` **F-24**. The facility walkthrough said a ConvertBack publishes *"the aggregate change"*. `MPTAmount` is a plaintext field on **every individual** ConvertBack, so retiring a facility in tranches publishes **every tranche size**. Corrected in the walkthrough; recorded because it survived that document's own `claims-check`.
   231	
   232	   *Falsified if:* the facility is built against a remote ledger endpoint while B-15's gate is shut, or if any part of it is described as hiding who transacts, when, or how often.
   233	
   234	8. **The web tier — a READ-ONLY, multi-role demonstration surface.** Bun · TanStack Start · Elysia · Tailwind · shadcn-idiom components. **SCOPED, BUILT AND EXERCISED 2026-08-28** — `web/api/` (Elysia, read-only) and `web/app/` (TanStack Start), **89 tests**, both type-checks clean, `make check-web-readonly` is the seventh gate, and the observer view was read against **live Devnet state** under the remote risk acceptance: two accounts at issuance `004625C9…FEE332CB25E8`, **`ConfidentialBalanceInbox` publicly identifiable as exactly zero on both** while `ConfidentialBalanceSpending` was non-zero on the same code path — **F-1 demonstrated live rather than asserted.** ⚠ **NOT under B-15's loopback condition**; the view is stamped `underB15Conditions: false` and says so on the page. **The scoping began with a correction**: ⚠ **`CLAUDE.md` told every new session that *"step 8 was written on 2026-08-26"* and it never was.** §6 had seven steps, §7 followed step 7 directly, and `grep -n "step 8"` over this document returned nothing. So the web tier had **no scope, no falsifier and no gate in the authority document** while the file every session reads first asserted that it did. **A scope note needs a re-check date exactly as much as an amendment claim does** — this document's own lesson about the `xrpl.js` blocker paragraph, landing one section over (`CLAIMS.md` **E-204**).
   235	
   236	   **What the step is, in one sentence: it renders what is already readable, and it adds no SIGNING authority.** ⚠⚠ **THIS READ *"and it adds no authority"* UNTIL 2026-09-02, AND THAT IS THE CLAIM `REVIEW-036` RETRACTED** (`CLAIMS.md` E-230): the tier served a decrypted issuer view and signer-local status, unauthenticated, to **any local uid**, out from behind `0600`/`0700` mode bits the project sets deliberately (E-228), until E-241 removed both on 2026-09-05. ⚠ This said *"serves"* until 2026-10-18. **A DISCLOSURE boundary moved. The signing claim is the sound one and is the one this step needs.** Four decisions were put to the project owner during scoping and ruled; they are recorded in **`ADR-0003`** and summarised here because §6 is where a step's shape lives.
   237	
   238	   - **D1 — the tier is read-only and never signs.** Browser → Elysia over localhost HTTP → a rippled node over JSON-RPC, plus **one** call into the signer's Unix socket: `Request::Status`, and nothing else. **Signing stays in the `wconf-signer` CLI**, where the holder confirms in the signer's own UI against parsed transaction fields (step 1's falsifier). ⚠⚠ ~~**This is the entire reason step 8 is buildable without an adversarial rotation** — it moves no trust boundary, because it introduces no new authority.~~ **STRUCK 2026-09-02 by `REVIEW-036`** — the rotation ran and ruled that reasoning unsound. **What is true: it introduces no new SIGNING authority**, and that is a narrower statement than *no boundary moves*. ⚠ **The rotation this sentence said was unnecessary is the one that falsified it.** The moment any path in this tier can reach a signing operation, it is a different design and §3's rotation requirement applies **before** implementation.
   239	   - **D2 — the browser wallet is prove-and-contrast, not a participant.** Connect a wallet, prove account control, show that account's **observer view**, and then show the capability matrix explaining what it cannot do. ⚠ **A browser wallet cannot perform a confidential operation**, and that is the demonstration rather than a gap: the operation needs the ElGamal secret **and** the XRPL account key; under the ruled keygen (`REVIEW-012`) the ElGamal secret is **derived from the master seed**; and a wallet extension exists precisely in order not to export seed material. **A fourth reason — that the extension's bundled codec does not know the XLS-0096 types — is asserted by no primary source this repository holds and is carried as a NAMED GAP, not as a premise.**
   240	   - **D3 — TanStack Start is kept, and the split is what makes it safe.** The capital-markets case is multi-role — issuer, auditor, supplier, facility operator, holder — and file-based routing with loaders is what lets those grow without a rewrite. SSR crosses no *new* boundary, because Elysia is already a local process reading holder data. ⚠ **The caveat makes the gate matter MORE, not less: server functions are exactly the one-line mechanism by which a later session adds a signing call.** Only the Elysia process may open the socket.
   241	   - **D4 — Ripple Custody is a research document and a simulated demo mode**, never an integration. This repository holds **no** Metaco Harmonize primary sources; every Harmonize specific is a dated named gap.
   242	
   243	   **What this step may touch:** public ledger state at a pinned validated index; the observer harness's view; the issuer-view decryption tool's output, given a demo issuer key handled as the secret it is; `Request::Status`; the facility's run records; and this repository's own documents.
   244	
   245	   **What it may never touch:** `Request::Pair`, `Request::Prepare`, any private key, any seed, any proof generation, and the Unix socket from anywhere but the Elysia process.
   246	
   247	   ⚠ **One thing verified at source during scoping, because D1's safety argument quotes it.** `signer/src/protocol.rs` documents `Status` as *"Public ledger read. Needs no grant and touches no secret."* **The second sentence is true and the first is false**: the handler at `signer/src/session.rs:248` reads **no ledger at all**. It returns the crate version, a hardcoded `keys_loaded: true`, and a note carrying the holder's address, the randomness ledger's generation id and its **entry count**. ⚠ **None of that is a secret and ⚠⚠ ~~none of it exceeds what the protocol already publishes~~ **STRUCK 2026-09-04, `CLAIMS.md` E-239 — the entry count includes cancelled confirmations, failed proofs and aborted attempts (`store.rs:753-756`), which no observer can reconstruct, and the API also returned the local socket path.** ⚠ E-241 withheld both on 2026-09-05; this said *"returns"* until 2026-10-18. P-4 and P-11 leave *who transacts and how often* public and publish neither of those — **but a comment that misdescribes what a call does is how E-200's secret went unwiped**, and this one sits under the sentence a ruling rests on. Corrected at the source rather than argued around (`CLAIMS.md` **E-205**).
   248	
   249	   **The gate is the load-bearing artifact of this step, and it is built before the UI.** `tools/check-web-readonly.ts`, wired into the `Makefile` beside the other six, asserting that no file under the web application imports a UDS client or names the socket path, that the Elysia process constructs `Status` and never `Pair` or `Prepare`, and that no route, loader or server function reaches a signing operation. ⚠ **Gates in this repository have been built to catch false passes and have false-passed** (E-165, E-168, E-182, E-198, E-203), so this one is verified by breaking it in both directions, its controls run through the real detector, and it **states what it does not check** in its own output.
   250	
   251	   **The capability matrix is the spine of the demonstration, not a footnote**, and it is derived from what the code supports rather than restated from a handover table. ⚠ **The `derived-from-master-seed` rows carry `REVIEW-012` condition 2's disclosure, which is RULED and not optional** — the master seed becomes a permanent decryption credential, and the warning for `lsfDisableMaster`, a regular key or a signer list must reach the user **before the seed is asked for**. ⚠ **Issue #10 is open and no code discharges it**: the derivation is specified in one repository document and is not a published standard, so the interface must not imply a holder with their seed and not this repository can reconstruct their key.
   252	
   253	   ⚠⚠ **THE FIRST BUILD VIOLATED THIS STEP'S OWN *"may never touch any private key"*, AND THE GATE COULD NOT SEE IT.** `web/api/issuer.ts` read the issuer secret and decrypted **per request** behind an unauthenticated `GET` — a decryption oracle, and `ADR-0003`'s own fourth rotation trigger, missed by the session that wrote the trigger (`CLAIMS.md` **E-212**). ⚠ **The gate asked what the tier could REACH; this was about what it HELD**, so a **part 4** now checks that no file under `web/` reads a secret environment variable, calls a decryption routine, or spawns a process. **The key lives in `tools/issuer-view-snapshot.ts` and the tier reads its output**, which is what the clause above always said. ⚠ **Two more of the same species**: the API listened on `0.0.0.0` because a bare port number forwards no hostname to `Bun.serve` (**measured on the LAN**), and the browser identity check ran in one loader rather than at the root (**E-213**).
   254	
   255	   *Falsified if:* any file under the web application opens a Unix domain socket, imports a UDS client, or names the signer socket path; or the Elysia process constructs `Request::Pair` or `Request::Prepare`; or any route, loader or server function reaches a signing operation. *Also falsified if:* **any process under `web/` reads a private key, performs a decryption, or spawns a subprocess**, or **the API binds to anything but loopback**. *Also falsified if:* the interface claims a holder is private from the issuer (P-9), presents a freshly-initialised balance as hidden when F-1 says it is publicly known to be exactly zero, describes the custody demo mode as an integration with any custody platform, or emits this project's `tapFailHard` shorthand — which is **not a real name** and has escaped into a public filing once already (E-156).
   256	
   257	9. **The hosted demo — a static, read-only page on a public URL.** **SCOPED 2026-10-19. NOT RULED, NOT BUILT. Rounds one and two of its rotation have run (`REVIEW-058`); round three, the last the owner's review policy allows, is owed.** `ADR-0004` is the decision record, `Proposed` at revision 3. It carries four decisions for the project owner, D-H1 to D-H4, each priced with a recommendation. ⚠ **A public deployment fires two of step 8's own conditions**: its falsifier *"the API binds to anything but loopback"*, and `ADR-0003`'s trigger 5, egress. The hosted page is public by design, so step 8's loopback property does not carry over to it, and this step's falsifiers replace it.
   258	
   259	   **What the step is, in one sentence, if D-H1 is ruled as recommended:** prerendered static pages, built from one pinned snapshot of public Devnet state with the reader the local tier already uses, each naming that ledger index and the date, with no code of this project running on a server per request and nothing sent by a visitor's browser except page loads to the host. ⚠ **This said the visitor's browser would read Devnet over WebSocket until `REVIEW-058` round one**, which found that reuse failing in a browser and pricing a second reader, and found that revision 1's claim that the gate could enumerate egress was false (`CLAIMS.md` E-328).
   260	
   261	   **It is a second build target, not a replacement.** Step 8's local tier stays as it is. Its signer panel and `/api-doc` page are absent from the hosted build.
   262	
   263	   **What this step may touch:** public Devnet ledger state, read at build time (or, under D-H1's H1, from the visitor's browser over the one named WebSocket endpoint); files built from a clean checkout of the public repository at a named commit, with its locked dependencies, named build variables only and no source maps; and one published issuer snapshot, only if D-H3 rules it.
   264	
   265	   **What it may never touch:** any server-side request handler; any socket client or the signer's socket path; any secret, seed or private key, the demo issuer key included; any decryption routine in the shipped output; any input asking a visitor for a secret; any network but Devnet; any second ledger endpoint, as a fallback or otherwise; any analytics, toolbar or third-party script; and any account a visitor chooses, carried in a URL the host receives.
   266	
   267	   **Its gate is a hosted part of `tools/check-web-readonly.ts`, run over the UPLOADED OUTPUT DIRECTORY and its routing configuration rather than the source**, verified by breaking it in both directions. `ADR-0004` lists what it must check. ⚠ It cannot check egress, and `check-no-secrets` has no directory mode yet, so the scan it relies on does not exist (`REVIEW-058` R1-9).
   268	
   269	   *Falsified if:* the shipped output contains a server function, an API route, a serverless function or middleware, in its files or its routing configuration; or contains a socket client, the signer socket path, a decryption routine or a secret-shaped value; or contains any file the provenance rule does not admit; or asks a visitor for a seed, a key or a secret; or permits a connection to anything but its named targets; or renders a ledger value without the risk wording, the network check and the ledger index it was read at; or renders a failed or negative read that cannot be placed in the pinned ledger as an empty account, or substitutes another source; or carries a visitor's lookup in a URL the host receives; or publishes an issuer figure D-H3 has not ruled; or ships bytes or headers other than the ones the gate passed; or does not render the commit it was built from and the gate's verdict on it. *Also falsified if:* any of step 8's wording falsifiers holds on the hosted page.
   270	
   271	## 7. What this architecture must never claim
   272	
   273	Beyond the standing prohibitions in `CLAIMS.md`, which govern:
   274	
   275	- That it hides **who** transacts, with whom, when, or how often. It hides amounts (P-4, P-11).
   276	- That a holder is private from the issuer (P-9).
   277	- That anything is secure, audited, non-custodial or production-ready (P-5).
   278	- That keys can be rotated or recovered (P-10).
   279	- That the local-signer boundary stops an adversary with code execution (§3).
   280	- That a demo of the happy path is evidence of confidentiality. It is not — that is what the observer harness is for.
    20	
    21	That split is not a deployment choice. The two programs talk over a **Unix domain socket**, which no browser page can open by any web API — so the path from a compromised page to a spending operation is closed by the transport itself rather than by a permission check. The web tier may ask the signer for its status and nothing else.
    22	
    23	⚠ **What this does not buy:** it does not stop a hostile program running as you, on your machine. That adversary is in scope and was never going to be stopped there; the confirmation screen in the signer is what stands in its way.
    24	
    25	---
    26	
    27	## The five key sources, and why the differences matter
    28	
    29	The **capability matrix** is the spine of the demonstration. It answers one question per row: *what can this key source actually do?*
    30	
    31	- **A seed-derived key** (generated or pasted) can decrypt its own balance and produce proofs, because the viewing key is **derived from the account's master seed**. One secret to keep.
    32	
    33	  ⚠ **The disclosure attached to those rows is ruled, not advisory.** The master seed becomes a **permanent decryption credential**: anyone who ever holds it can decrypt every confidential balance the account has held, past and future. The protocol has **no key rotation and no key recovery**, so this cannot be undone. The demonstration shows that sentence *before* it asks for a seed, because that is the order the ruling requires.
    34	
    35	  ⚠ **And the derivation is not a published standard.** It is specified in one document in this repository. A holder with their seed and not this repository **cannot reconstruct their key.** That is an open issue and no code closes it.
    36	
    37	- **A standalone key** is an independent viewing key with its own backup. Two secrets to keep. It is the honest option when the master seed has been retired — and it is the row a custody platform lands on.
    38	
    39	- **A browser wallet** can do neither, and this is the sharpest thing in the demonstration. A confidential operation needs the viewing key **and** the signing key; the viewing key is derived from the master seed; and a wallet extension exists **in order not to release seed material**. So the operation cannot be assembled at all. It is not a permission being withheld — there is nothing to grant.
    40	
    41	- **A custody platform**, simulated, splits in two: it can sign, and it cannot evaluate a policy on an amount it cannot see.
    42	
    43	---
    44	
    45	## What an observer sees
    46	
    47	Point the observer view at any account and issuance. What comes back is what **anyone** can read from public ledger data, with no secret at all:
    48	
    49	- the account, and every counterparty it has transacted with;
    50	- every transaction's timing and frequency;
     1	import { createFileRoute } from "@tanstack/react-router";
     2	import { api } from "../api";
     3	import { Panel, Verdict } from "../components/Verdict";
     4

     6	  loader: () => api.capabilities(),
     7	  component: Wallet,
     8	});

    10	/**
    11	 * Prove-and-contrast. ⚠ The contrast is the lesson: a browser wallet cannot
    12	 * hold a confidential position, and the reasons are structural rather than
    13	 * missing features.
    14	 */
    15	function Wallet() {
    16	  const { rows, namedGaps } = Route.useLoaderData();
    17	  const wallet = rows.find((r: any) => r.id === "browser-wallet");
    18	
    19	  return (
    20	    <>
    21	      <Panel title="A browser wallet cannot hold a confidential position">
    22	        <p className="text-sm leading-relaxed mb-4">
    23	          This is not a feature gap in the three wallet APIs examined (Xaman, GemWallet,
    24	          Crossmark, checked 2026-08-28). ⚠ It is <em>not</em> a claim that no wallet API
    25	          could ever close it — an extension that derived or held an ElGamal key internally,
    26	          and exposed a prove-and-sign operation without exporting seed material, would.
    27	          Nothing observed does. Three reasons, each structural to the design as it stands:
    28	        </p>
    29	        <ol className="text-sm space-y-3 list-decimal pl-5">
    30	          <li>
    31	            A confidential operation needs <strong>two</strong> secrets — the ElGamal secret, to
    32	            decrypt the balance and produce the proof, and the XRPL account key, to sign. A wallet
    33	            supplies the second.
    34	          </li>
    35	          <li>
    36	            Under the ruled key generation, the ElGamal secret is <strong>derived from the master
    37	            seed</strong>. There is no other way to obtain it for a derived-key account.
    38	          </li>
    39	          <li>
    40	            A wallet extension exists <strong>in order not to release seed material</strong>. That is
    41	            the product, not a limitation of its API.
    42	          </li>
    43	        </ol>
    44	        <p className="text-sm mt-4">
    45	          So the operation cannot be assembled at all. The wallet is not being denied permission —
    46	          there is nothing to grant.
    47	        </p>
    48	      </Panel>
    49	
    50	      <Panel title="What a wallet CAN do">

    54	            <div>

    65	                Which is enough to show that account’s observer view — the same view anyone with the
    66	                address can read.
    67	              </p>
    68	            </div>
    69	          </div>
    70	        </div>
    71	      </Panel>
    72

    76	          CLAIMS.md E-206. Recorded because an earlier draft of this project’s reasoning led with
    77	          exactly this point, and it is a version fact an upstream release could close at any time.
    78	          The three reasons above cannot be closed by a codec release. A wallet that held the
    79	          viewing key and produced the proofs itself would close them, and none observed does.
    80	        </p>

     1	/**
     2	 * Known Devnet accounts, so the demonstration does not open on an empty form.
     3	 *
     4	 * ⚠⚠ **PUBLIC LEDGER DATA ONLY, AND THE OMISSION IS DELIBERATE.** An account
     5	 * address and an `MPTokenIssuanceID` are readable by anyone with a block
     6	 * explorer — that is `CLAIMS.md` P-4 and P-11, and rendering them gives nothing
     7	 * away that the protocol was hiding.
     8	 *
     9	 * ⚠ **`/tmp/wconf/state.json` IS NOT READ AND MUST NOT BE.** That file holds
    10	 * throwaway account seeds and ElGamal secrets. The addresses below were
    11	 * transcribed from it by hand, once, and **the secrets stayed there** — a
    12	 * read-only rendering tier that opens a file full of seed material has become
    13	 * something else, whatever it does with the contents. If this list needs to
    14	 * grow, transcribe the public fields again rather than teaching this process to
    15	 * read that file.
    16	 *
    17	 * ⚠ **It is a convenience, not a registry.** The observer and issuer views take
    18	 * any account and issuance; this only saves pasting 34 and 48 characters.
    19	 */
    20

   694	    /// Every answer must say it came from the ledger that was asked for.
   695	    ///
   696	    /// ⚠ **Asking is not checking.** `REVIEW-016`'s Codex leg found this file
   697	    /// claiming *"one pinned numeric ledger index"* while verifying only that
   698	    /// `ledger_entry` called itself validated — so a node could answer
   699	    /// `account_info` from one ledger and `ledger_entry` from another, and the
   700	    /// torn read this module exists to prevent would be assembled and labelled
   701	    /// with the first index.
   702	    fn came_from(&self, command: &str, r: &Value, ledger_index: u32) -> Result<(), LedgerError> {
   703	        if r.get("validated").and_then(Value::as_bool) != Some(true) {
   704	            return Err(LedgerError::Desynchronised {
   705	                command: command.into(),
   706	                asked: ledger_index,
   707	                answered: None,
   708	            });
   709	        }
   710	        let answered = u32_field(r.get("ledger_index"), &format!("{command}.ledger_index"))?;
   711	        if answered != ledger_index {
   712	            return Err(LedgerError::Desynchronised {
   713	                command: command.into(),
   714	                asked: ledger_index,
   715	                answered: Some(answered),
   716	            });
   717	        }
   718	        Ok(())
   719	    }

   948	        }
   949	        self.came_from("ledger_entry", &r, ledger_index)?;
   950	        let node = r
   951	            .get("node")
   952	            .cloned()
   953	            .ok_or_else(|| LedgerError::Malformed("ledger_entry returned no `node`".into()))?;
   954	        // The node must be the kind of object that was asked for. Without this
   955	        // a node could return an `MPTokenIssuance` where an `MPToken` was
   956	        // requested, and `field_hex` would read whichever fields happened to
   957	        // have the right names and lengths.
   958	        let kind = node.get("LedgerEntryType").and_then(Value::as_str).unwrap_or("");
   959	        if kind != expect_type {
   960	            return Err(LedgerError::Malformed(format!(
   961	                "ledger_entry was asked for a {expect_type} and returned a `{kind}`"
   962	            )));
   963	        }
   964	        Ok(Some(node))
   965	    }

# REVIEW-058 round two, leg A — the fixes to round one, before any code

You are an adversarial reviewer. Round one of this review found defects in a
proposed design for a public, read-only demo page, and in shipped code the design
reuses. The author fixed them. **Your subject is those fixes**, which nobody has
reviewed. In this project the second round exists because fixing something and
describing the fix are separate acts with separate failure modes: a fix applied
to the line a finding cited, while a copy of the same sentence stands elsewhere,
is the most common defect here. Agreement is not useful. A finding that a fix
holds is useful only if you tried to break it and say how.

## STEP 0 — do this before anything else, and report the results first

1. Run `curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' https://github.com` and

2. Run `pwd` and `git log --oneline -1`. The working copy is a clone at commit
   `629f21621c723cd7f61afec032c09248760b3ddf`. Work only inside this clone.

⚠ THIS RUN IS NOT UNDER B-15's STEP-7 CONDITIONS.

  The facility's ledger source is REMOTE and is trusted for amount
  confidentiality. CLAIMS.md F-23 is EXECUTED, not theoretical: a source that
  lies about a destination's HolderEncryptionKey makes the signer publish the
  amount encrypted under the liar's key, permanently, in public. TLS does not
  address it — it authenticates the channel to a node someone chose.

  B-15's step-7 answer was AMENDED by the project owner on 2026-08-25 to
  extend REVIEW-019's named risk acceptance to this demo. That amendment, like
  the ruling it amends, has had NO adversarial review.

  What a run under this hatch CAN establish: that the transactions serialise,
  that rippled accepts them, that the balances decrypt to what the book
  predicts, and what a full-ledger observer sees.

  ⚠ It does NOT establish XLS-0096 §10.5's state changes. This sentence said it
  did, while run-facility.ts's own header said it did not — REVIEW-023 found the
  pair. The driver checks the outstanding-amount delta on the float conversion
  and reconciles decrypted balances; there are no per-movement assertions over
  the public amount or the balance version. CLAIMS.md B-18.

  What it CANNOT establish: anything whatever about the loopback obligation.
  Do not record a run under this hatch as evidence that the facility keeps it.

describe("construction", () => {
  test("refuses a Dedicated Account that is the issuer — a PROTOCOL rule, not a preference", async () => {
    // An issuer cannot convert its own issuance into a confidential balance;
    // the ledger refuses it outright. That is the reason this design has a
    // separate account at all, and stating it as a style choice would be a
    // claim about rippled that rippled does not make.
    await expect(facilityWith({}, SEED_A)).rejects.toThrow(/REFUSED_ISSUER_IS_DEDICATED/);
  });

  test("refuses a party whose seed does not control its claimed address", async () => {
    const { server, endpoint } = nodeWith({});
    const ledger = await LoopbackLedger.open(endpoint);
    const { book, cleanup } = tempBook();
    try {
      expect(
        () =>
          new Facility(ledger, new FailHardSubmitter(ledger), book, {
            issuanceId: ISSUANCE,
            bank: party("bank", SEED_A),
            // A claimed address that belongs to someone else entirely — and
            // NOT the bank's, or the issuer-is-dedicated guard would fire first
            // and this test would pass for the wrong reason.
            dedicated: { label: "dedicated", seed: SEED_B, address: addressOfSeed(SEED_C) },
            fullRange: 100_000,
            ledgerEndpoint: endpoint,
            workRoot: "/tmp/wconf-facility-test",
            autoConfirm: true,
          }),
      ).toThrow(/REFUSED_SEED_ADDRESS_MISMATCH/);
    } finally {
      server.stop(true);
      cleanup();
    }
  });

  test("refuses a non-loopback ledger endpoint for the signer's own reads", async () => {
    // Two processes read the same ledger for two different reasons, and B-15's
    // ruling covers both. The signer's reads are the ones F-23 is actually
    // about.
    const { server, endpoint } = nodeWith({});
    const ledger = await LoopbackLedger.open(endpoint);
    const { book, cleanup } = tempBook();
    try {
      // ⚠ **The refusal depends on `WCONF_ACCEPT_REMOTE_LEDGER_RISK` being
      // unset, and this test owns that rather than hoping.** `REVIEW-023` round
      // two ran the suite with the acceptance sentence exported and got 76 pass
      // / 3 fail; this was one of the three. A test that asserts an
      // environment-dependent outcome must own the environment.
      await withHatch(undefined, async () => {
        expect(
          () =>
            new Facility(ledger, new FailHardSubmitter(ledger), book, {
              issuanceId: ISSUANCE,
              bank: party("bank", SEED_A),
              dedicated: party("dedicated", SEED_B),
              fullRange: 100_000,
              ledgerEndpoint: HOSTILE_ENDPOINTS.find((h) =>
                h.why.includes("every other harness"),
              )!.endpoint,
              workRoot: "/tmp/wconf-facility-test",
              autoConfirm: true,
            }),
        ).toThrow(/REFUSED_NOT_LOOPBACK/);
      });
    } finally {
      server.stop(true);
      cleanup();
    }
  });
});

describe("the ElGamal key each party reads its own balance with", () => {
  test("comes from the ruled derivation and is checked against the address", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({});
    try {
      const key = f.facility.elgamalOf(f.bank);
      // REVIEW-012 §6's V1 vector. If this changes, either the derivation moved
      // or the seed did, and both are worth stopping for.
      expect(key.pk).toBe("02295AD17728DBD75FAB16EE3A38E7D1ED96908DD7E530C90BD8965AF0A7E71246");
      expect(key.address).toBe(f.bank.address);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });
});

describe("adopting a supplier an earlier run enrolled", () => {
  // ⚠ `adoptEnrolledSupplier` was ADDED as a round-one fix and shipped with no
  // test — REVIEW-022 round two found that, and a fix nobody can falsify is a
  // fix on the same footing as the cast it replaced.
  const supplier = party("supplier", SEED_C);
  const derived = deriveElGamalKey(SEED_C).pk;

  test("adopts a supplier whose registered key is the derived one", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({
      [supplier.address]: { MPTAmount: "1", HolderEncryptionKey: derived },
    });
    try {
      await f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(supplier);
      expect(f.facility.supplierList.map((p) => p.address)).toEqual([supplier.address]);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("refuses an account that carries no key at all", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({});
    try {
      await expect(f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(supplier)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_NOT_ENROLLED/,
      );
      expect(f.facility.supplierList).toEqual([]);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("refuses an account whose registered key is NOT the derived one", async () => {
    // ⚠ This is the case the cast it replaced could not see: a position the
    // facility would track and could never decrypt.
    const f = await facilityWith({
      [supplier.address]: { MPTAmount: "1", HolderEncryptionKey: "02" + "CD".repeat(32) },
    });
    try {
      await expect(f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(supplier)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_KEY_NOT_DERIVED/,
      );
      expect(f.facility.supplierList).toEqual([]);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("refuses a party whose seed does not control its claimed address", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({});
    try {
      const liar = { label: "liar", seed: SEED_C, address: addressOfSeed(SEED_B) };
      await expect(f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(liar)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_SEED_ADDRESS_MISMATCH/,
      );
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });
});

describe("refusals before a transaction is built", () => {
  const supplier = party("supplier", SEED_C);
  const supplierEntry = {
    MPTAmount: "1",
    HolderEncryptionKey: deriveElGamalKey(SEED_C).pk,
    MPTokenIssuanceID: ISSUANCE,
  };

  test("a movement against a party the facility does not know is refused", async () => {
    // ⚠ `confidential()` hands the signer a SEED while the book and the ledger
    // lookup use the ADDRESS. A `Party` the facility never enrolled can carry
    // any pairing of the two, so a transaction signed by one account would be
    // booked against another. Found by REVIEW-022's Codex leg.
    const f = await facilityWith({});
    try {
      f.book.record({ kind: "convert", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 1000, hash: "h1" });
      f.book.record({ kind: "merge", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 1000, hash: "m1" });
      await expect(f.facility.drawdown(supplier, 10)).rejects.toThrow(/REFUSED_UNKNOWN_PARTY/);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("a movement to a destination with no registered key is an IMPOSSIBILITY, not a rejection", async () => {
    // ⚠ There is no key to encrypt to, so the transaction cannot be built. A
    // facility that reported this as "the ledger refused it" would be
    // describing a round trip that never happens.
    //
    // The destination here is the **Dedicated Account before it has converted**,
    // which is the case that is actually reachable: a supplier only enters the
    // facility's own list by way of an enrolment that required their key.
    const f = await facilityWith({ [supplier.address]: supplierEntry });
    try {
      await f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(supplier);
      f.book.record({ kind: "convert", account: supplier.address, amount: 50, hash: "h1" });
      f.book.record({ kind: "merge", account: supplier.address, amount: 50, hash: "m1" });
      await expect(f.facility.repay(supplier, 10)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_DESTINATION_NOT_REGISTERED/,
      );
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("a drawdown larger than the tracked balance is refused before any entropy is drawn", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({
      [supplier.address]: supplierEntry,
    });
    try {
      await f.facility.adoptEnrolledSupplier(supplier);
      // ⚠ convert lands in the INBOX; the merge is what makes it spendable.
      f.book.record({ kind: "convert", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 100, hash: "h1" });
      f.book.record({ kind: "merge", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 100, hash: "m1" });
      await expect(f.facility.drawdown(supplier, 101)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_INSUFFICIENT_CONFIDENTIAL_BALANCE/,
      );
      // The control: one unit less passes every FACILITY guard. Without it the
      // assertion above passes against a facility that refuses everything —
      // which is the shape REVIEW-016 round two found in two of its own new
      // tests, where the case named was never the reason the test failed.
      //
      // ⚠ It asserts on the TYPE, not on the absence of one string. Asserting
      // only `not.toContain(...)` passed for the wrong reason — the signer
      // cannot sign against this fake node, so any failure at all satisfied it,
      // including one thrown before the guards ran. REVIEW-022's Gemini leg
      // found that, and its falsification was exact: an unconditional throw in
      // `confidentialTransfer` would have satisfied both assertions. Every
      // facility guard throws `FacilityRefusal`, so "not a FacilityRefusal"
      // means the guards were passed and the failure came from further down.
      const boundary = await f.facility.drawdown(supplier, 100).catch((e) => e);
      expect(boundary).toBeInstanceOf(Error);
      expect(boundary).not.toBeInstanceOf(FacilityRefusal);
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("retiring more than the Dedicated Account holds is refused", async () => {
    const f = await facilityWith({});
    try {
      f.book.record({ kind: "convert", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 50, hash: "h1" });
      f.book.record({ kind: "merge", account: f.dedicated.address, amount: 50, hash: "m1" });
      await expect(f.facility.retire(51)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_INSUFFICIENT_CONFIDENTIAL_BALANCE/,
      );
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });

  test("enrolling an account that already carries a HolderEncryptionKey is refused", async () => {
    // ⚠ Registration happens exactly once and the protocol forbids repeating
    // it. Discovering that by submitting is discovering it in public.
    const f = await facilityWith({
      [supplier.address]: { MPTAmount: "5", HolderEncryptionKey: "02CD" },
    });
    try {
      await expect(f.facility.enrolSupplier(supplier, 5)).rejects.toThrow(
        /REFUSED_ALREADY_REGISTERED/,
      );
    } finally {
      f.stop();
    }
  });
});

docs/architecture/ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md:259 uses, each naming that ledger index and the date, with no code of this project running on a server per request and nothing sent by a visitor's browser except page loads to the host. ⚠ **This said the visitor's browser wo
docs/decisions/ADR-0004-hosted-demo-trust-boundary.md:92 ** The deployment is static files. ⚠ Revision 1 said *"no code of ours"*, and the page's own script runs in every visitor's browser (`REVIEW-058` B1-10). - **Every ledger value is a dated reading.** The build opens one snapshot at one validated index, with the reader the local tier already uses, and reads every entry each page needs from it; every page names that index and the date. Nothing refreshes it until the next build. ⚠ Revision 2 said the build read Devnet *"once per page"*, which is neither one read nor one snapshot per page (`REVIEW-058` B2-3). - **The visitor's browser sends nothing anywhere but Vercel.** The pages are fixed: one per demo account, with no input, so no lookup travels in a URL. - **The capability matrix, the custody simulation and the wallet page are static.** They are functions of this repository's own text (`web/api/capability.ts`, `web/api/custody-sim.ts`). - **The issuer view is D-H3**, an owner decision. **What the hosted output may contain, by provenance.** Files built from a clean checkout of the public repository at a named commit, with its locked dependencies, with no environment variable admitted to the build except those named in the build script, and with no source maps. ⚠ Revision 1 described the output as *"the static output of the public repository's own source"* and treated that as a boundary. M11 shows a build input outside the source reaching the output, so provenance is something the build has to enforce and the gate has to check, not a description (`REVIEW-058` R1-3). **The build machine is trusted, and that has to be a choice.** Installing dependencies and building runs third-party code with the build user's file access. An inventory of the output cannot show that the build never read a private store or a credential. Build where neither the private stores (`~/.wconf`, `/tmp/wconf`) nor the deployment token is reachable, and bring the token in only for the upload step; or accept the build machine's trust by name (`REVIEW-058` R2-10). **What the hosted tier causes to be sent, and to whom.** Each page load goes to Vercel, which sees the visitor's IP address and the page requested. Under H3 that is all. Under H1 the visitor's browser also connects to Ripple's Devnet node and sends every account it looks up. ⚠ **Revision 1 said trigger 5 fired only for what is put into the bundle, and that the gate could enumerate it. Both halves were false.** Visitors' requests are egress no bundle contains, and `ADR-0003` itself says its gate does not check what the tier emits or to whom. The gate can enumerate the uploaded files; it cannot enumerate egress (`REVIEW-058` B1-4, and the repository leg's closing finding). **Trigger 4, the decryption oracle.** No request reaches a decryption under any D-H3 option: A and B decrypt nothing at request time, and C adds a verifier. That is narrower than revision 1's *"cannot fire: there is no request handler"*. A static file server answers requests, and a pre-decrypted figure served to everyone is the result leaving, which is E-212's lesson. That is why D-H3 is a ruling. **Hosted exclusions.** The local tier's signer panel and its loader call (`web/app/src/routes/index.tsx:12-15,72-85`) are absent, not merely the signer page; ⚠ revision 1 called it a page. The `/api-doc` page describes an API that does not exist in the hosted build and is absent too. Two false sentences found on the local tier by round one are corrected at source: the front page promised the issuer view showed the holder *"decrypted"*, and the wallet page said three reasons ~~*"cannot be closed by any release"*~~ after saying a wallet that held the key would close them (`REVIEW-058` R1-4, `CLAIMS.md` E-328). ⚠ Round two found the same permanence claim surviving in the capability matrix, which the wallet page renders (`web/api/capability.ts`, the browser-wallet row), and it is now scoped to the wallets examined (R2-2, E-329). ## What the hosted surface may touch, and may never touch **May touch:** public Devnet ledger state, read from one pinned snapshot at build time, or under H1 from the visitor's browser over the one named WebSocket endpoint; static content built as the provenance rule above says; and, only if D-H3 rules it, one published snapshot file. **May never touch:** any server-side request handler; any socket client or the signer's socket path; any secret, seed or private key, the demo issuer key included; any decryption routine in the shipped output; any input that asks a visitor for a seed, a key or any secret (`REVIEW-058` S-1); any network but Devnet; any second ledger endpoint, as a fallback or otherwise; any analytics, toolbar or third-party script; and any account or issuance a visitor chooses, carried in a URL the host receives (B1-2). ## The owner's decisions ### D-H1. The hosting shape | option | cost | what it buys | |---|---|---| | **H3. Prerendered pages** *(recommended)* | a build step that opens one pinned snapshot with the existing reader, naming its endpoint and the remote-risk acceptance input in the build's allowlist; replacing the application's runtime dependency on the API, because every route today passes the root's `/api/meta` check and the wallet and custody loaders call the API, with data baked at build time and checked by loading each page directly and by navigating, with no API running (`REVIEW-058` R2-9); a check that each demo account exists, so a reset, a missing token and a failed read render differently; a rebuild cadence the owner names, or an accepted indefinite snapshot; a visitor cannot enter an account | no visitor request leaves Vercel; no second ledger reader; no WebSocket, reconnection or `Buffer` problem; every value is a dated reading, P-3's habit. ⚠ The build still reads one node, so D-H2's first paragraph still applies | | H1. Static site, the visitor's browser reads Devnet | a WebSocket transport under `Snapshot`, after `loopback.ts` is refactored to separate its transport (`REVIEW-058` B1-9); a browser-safe replacement for the harness's `Buffer` helpers; snapshot integrity across reconnects; a Content Security Policy that admits the node; no loader cache reused across navigation | a visitor can look up any Devnet account. ⚠ Their IP and every lookup go to Ripple's node. Port 51233 is not 443, and networks that block other ports will fail the page (B1-1); whether a Devnet WebSocket endpoint on 443 exists has **not been searched** | | H2. Vercel functions running the Elysia API against Devnet over JSON-RPC, with SSR | the API on the public internet: CORS for the deployed origin, rate limiting, caching, function logs holding visitors' IPs and queries, and billing exposure on the owner's account | the existing reader unchanged, and visitors' IPs hidden from Ripple's node. ⚠ Every route added later runs our code per request, which is trigger 4's shape | **Why H3.** Revision 1 recommended H1, and round one priced what H1 costs. Its 
docs/decisions/ADR-0004-hosted-demo-trust-boundary.md:310 server-side code of ours runs per request, so there is nothing of ours to rate limit. ⚠ This said *"nothing of ours"* in revision 2, a copy of the sentence round one corrected two sections up (`REVIEW-058` R2-1). Static hosting still has costs: page loads and transfer count against the owner's Vercel plan, and a flood of them can exhaust a free plan's quota or bill a paid one. Name the plan, its limits, a spend cap if the plan offers one, and who acts when the page goes down (`REVIEW-058` R1-11). Under H1 the node's limits are Ripple's, and the page must render a refused, failed or rate-limited read as a named failure, never as an empty account. Under H2 all of it becomes ours. **Copies.** The bundle is public and anyone can host a copy that reads a node of their choosing. Nothing prevents that. The page names its canonical address and the repository, and that is the whole defence. ## Caching Under H3 the pages are cached, dated observations, deliberately: each names its ledger index and date and none claims to be current. ⚠ `REVIEW-058c` said H3 made *"Ledger values are not cached"* true by construction, and it does the opposite (R2-6). ⚠ Revision 1 said *"Ledger values are not cached"* without a mechanism, and under H1 TanStack Router's loaders keep data across navigation by default (`REVIEW-058` B1-8). Under H1 the build must disable loader caching for ledger reads, and a test must show that returning to a view reads again. ## Rotation triggers `ADR-0003`'s five triggers apply to the hosted build unchanged. Four more are specific to hosting: 1. **Anything that runs code of ours per request**: a serverless function, edge middleware, or on-demand rendering. That is H2, and it needs its own rotation.

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/serde-rs/json/v1.0.151/src/number.rs 
 pub fn as_u64(&self) -> Option<u64> {
        #[cfg(not(feature = "arbitrary_precision"))]
        match self.n {
            N::PosInt(n) => Some(n),
            N::NegInt(_) | N::Float(_) => None,
        }
        #[cfg(feature = "arbitrary_precision")]
        self.n.parse().ok()
    }

    /// Represents the number as f64 if possible. Returns None otherwise.
    pub fn as_f64(&self) -> Option<f64> {
        #[cfg(not(feature = "arbitrary_precision"))]
        match self.n {
            N::PosInt(n) => Some(n as f64),
            N::NegInt(n) => Some(n as f64),
            N::Float(n) => Some(n),
        }
        #[cfg(feature = "arbitrary_precision")]

 pub fn from_f64(f: f64) -> Option<Number> {
        if f.is_finite() {
            let n = {
                #[cfg(not(feature = "arbitrary_precision"))]
                {
                    N::Float(f)
                }
                #[cfg(feature = "arbitrary_precision")]
                {
                    zmij::Buffer::new().format_finite(f).to_owned()
                }
            };
            Some(Number { n })
        } else {
            None
        }
    }

FILE web/app/src/api.ts
60:  * ⚠ The result is cached for the life of the page: the check exists to catch a
61:  * **wrong service on the port**, which does not change between navigations, and
62:  * re-fetching `/meta` on every route would be a cost without a property.
63:  */
64: let identity: Promise<void> | null = null;
65: 
66: export function assertOurApi(): Promise<void> {
67:   identity ??= (async () => {
68:     const meta = await get<any>("/meta");
69:     if (meta?.service !== "wconf-read-only-api") {
70:       throw new Error(
71:         `The proxied port is answering, but it is not the W-CONF read-only API ` +
72:         `(service: ${JSON.stringify(meta?.service)}). Refusing to render its data.`,
73:       );
74:     }
75:   })().catch((e) => {
76:     // ⚠ A failed check must not be cached as "checked". Reset so the next
77:     // navigation retries rather than inheriting a stale rejection.
78:     identity = null;
79:     throw e;
80:   });
81:   return identity;
82: }

docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:27: ## The five key sources, and why the differences matter
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:28: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:29: The **capability matrix** is the spine of the demonstration. It answers one question per row: *what can this key source actually do?*
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:30: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:31: - **A seed-derived key** (generated or pasted) can decrypt its own balance and produce proofs, because the viewing key is **derived from the account's master seed**. One secret to keep.
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:32: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:33:   ⚠ **The disclosure attached to those rows is ruled, not advisory.** The master seed becomes a **permanent decryption credential**: anyone who ever holds it can decrypt every confidential balance the account has held, past and future. The protocol has **no key rotation and no key recovery**, so this cannot be undone. The demonstration shows that sentence *before* it asks for a seed, because that is the order the ruling requires.
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:34: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:35:   ⚠ **And the derivation is not a published standard.** It is specified in one document in this repository. A holder with their seed and not this repository **cannot reconstruct their key.** That is an open issue and no code closes it.
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:36: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:37: - **A standalone key** is an independent viewing key with its own backup. Two secrets to keep. It is the honest option when the master seed has been retired — and it is the row a custody platform lands on.
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:38: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:39: - **A browser wallet** can do neither, and this is the sharpest thing in the demonstration. A confidential operation needs the viewing key **and** the signing key; the viewing key is derived from the master seed; and a wallet extension exists **in order not to release seed material**. So the operation cannot be assembled at all. It is not a permission being withheld — there is nothing to grant.
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:40: 
docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-28.md:41: - **A custody platform**, simulated, splits in two: it can sign, and it cannot evaluate a policy on an amount it cannot see.

fn parse_number(&mut self, positive: bool, significand: u64) -> Result<ParserNumber> {
        Ok(match tri!(self.peek_or_null()) {
            b'.' => ParserNumber::F64(tri!(self.parse_decimal(positive, significand, 0))),
            b'e' | b'E' => ParserNumber::F64(tri!(self.parse_exponent(positive, significand, 0))),
            _ => {
                if positive {
                    ParserNumber::U64(significand)
                } else {

**R3-3 | HIGH | The reader still does not match Rust’s strict numeric contract | Evidence:** `facility/src/loopback.ts:265–267` checks the already-parsed JavaScript number. Both decimal wire forms above become `4800000`, including the mathematically different fractional value. Rust uses `Value::as_u64` plus checked `u32` conversion at `signer/src/ledger.rs:553–561,702–710`. The pinned [serde_json parser](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/serde-rs/json/v1.0.151/src/de.rs) classifies decimal forms as floats, and [`as_u64`](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/serde-rs/json/v1.0.151/src/number.rs) refuses floats. ADR:298–301, `CLAIMS.md:389`, and 058d:46 nevertheless claim parity. **Discharge:** either enforce Rust’s unsigned-32-bit JSON-integer representation before number coercion, with positive/negative decimal and range controls, **or explicitly document the weaker contract everywhere parity is claimed**, as R2-A:75 allowed.
